And the profile's plan-first is **suppressed with an explanatory line** — not silently applied — when `record_plan` can't register (`--no-builtin-tools`, no resolved `.agents/` directory, or `record_plan` in either disable list), because `/replan` only revokes a plan and never grants one: the gate would deny every mutating call for the life of the session with nothing able to clear it. Explicit operator intent in that same situation is honored, with a loud warning naming the specific blocker. Every start prints the resolved posture (`tools=default−bash`, `plan-first: on (task profile)`). Closes [#160](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/160).

- watchdog/agent/config: the runaway-behavior watchdog now has a **`feedback` mode that tells the model what it is doing**, and `enforce` includes it. The watchdog's one shipped signal fires when the agent makes five byte-identical tool calls in a row — and every existing mode routed that observation to a reader who cannot act on it: `warn` logs a line for an operator who, in the unattended runs [#642](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/642) just defaulted to a backstop, is a pod log nobody is tailing; `enforce` halts the process. The one party in a position to *stop looping* — the model choosing the next tool call — was never told. The modes are now a ladder (`off` < `warn` < `feedback` < `enforce`), each including the one before it, and options are appended additively at both wiring sites (`cmd/core-agent`, `compose.SessionFactoryDeps`) so a stronger rung can't drift from the weaker one it contains. Under `feedback` and above, the next turn's prompt is prefixed with a `[watchdog]` block naming the signal and what to do differently, ahead of the pending-alert and inbox prepends so it reads first. `enforce` implying feedback is the load-bearing half: an enforce halt is cleared by an operator reset ([#666](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/666)), and that reset resumes a model whose context still ends in the loop it was halted for, so without the injection the reset is a treadmill — the same five calls, the same halt, one operator round-trip later. `ResetWatchdog` therefore clears the halt but deliberately keeps the queued observation, and a halt restored from the eventlog after a restart ([#643](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/643)) synthesizes the observation from the persisted reason, since the in-memory queue died with the process and durable-halt-without-durable-correction is half a property. `watchdog.Alert` splits into an operator-facing `Reason` (may name `/interrupt`, `--max-turn-cost-usd`) and a model-facing `Guidance` that names no operator affordance the model doesn't have — a correction that tells the model to "consider `/interrupt`" is noise at best; `FormatFeedback` falls back to `Reason` so a third-party `Signal` that sets no `Guidance` still produces feedback rather than being silently inert. `warn` is deliberately unchanged, because altering it would silently rewrite the context of every operator already running it. The queue is bounded (4 alerts, oldest dropped), nothing is queued while the mode is below `feedback` so a later flip can't deliver a stale backlog, and the block is framed as an automated observation the user cannot see — it is not a trust boundary (a prompt can contain the literal string, as with `[Inbox]`). This is a correction, not a backstop: nothing halts a model that reads the block and loops anyway, so unattended runs still want `enforce` — which now gets both. Closes [#159](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/159).

- watchdog: a **second loop detector**, plus argument canonicalization — the two evasions the v1 detector named in its own docstring and could not catch. `repeated-tool-call` requires a *consecutive* run of one call, so wedging any second call into the loop hid it; the live GKE UAT loop that survived an operator `stop` *and* `/interrupt` ([#622](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/622)) was exactly that shape, `list_agents → check_agent` forever. New `alternating-tool-cycle` scans the recent call history for a 2–4 call sequence repeated three times with byte-identical arguments each lap, and is wired into `NewDefaultWatchdog` alongside the repeat detector — both `Critical`, so both halt under `--watchdog=enforce` and both reach the model under `--watchdog=feedback` ([#159](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/159)). Arguments are now compared path-canonicalized rather than as literal strings: values under path-shaped keys are cleaned, so `main.go`, `./main.go` and `dir/../main.go` are one call, and the consecutive detector additionally folds a genuine component-boundary suffix (`/workspace/main.go` ≡ `main.go`) — an agent re-reading one file under three spellings is as stuck as one re-reading it under the same spelling. The generalization is deliberately narrow in both directions, because a Critical alert halts the agent: basename matching is rejected (`a/doc.go` vs `b/doc.go` would false-positive on every repo with repeated filenames), non-path values are never normalized even when they look like paths (a `grep` pattern, a `bash` command), two laps is not a cycle (read-grep-read-grep is ordinary exploration), and a block of one repeated call is left to the repeat detector so one behavior never raises two alerts. Cycle alerts are fingerprinted rotation-invariantly, so a loop that presents as `a → b` on one call and `b → a` on the next still gets one notice rather than one per call. The known false positive — a hand-rolled polling loop written as alternating calls — is what `wait_and_verify` ([#648](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/648)) exists to replace, and an embedder can construct `DefaultWatchdog` with their own signal list. Semantic ("same question, different phrasing") detection stays v3.0 scope. Closes [#649](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/649).

#### Bug or Regression
- pricing/tui/taskclass/usage: **the model tables are now one table with three narrowings, instead of four hand-maintained lists that had drifted apart.** `pkg/pricing`'s builtin rates, the `/model` picker, the task-class tier defaults and `usage`'s context-window map each enumerated models independently, and each had aged at its own rate. The picker was the visible casualty: six of its nineteen entries (`gemini-2.5-pro`, `gemini-2.5-flash`, `gemini-3-flash-preview`, `gemini-3.1-pro-preview`, `gemini-3.1-pro-preview-customtools`, `gemini-3.1-flash-image-preview`) resolved to no rate at all, which shows as `$—` in the TUI and, far worse, makes `--max-turn-cost-usd` / `--max-session-cost-usd` **silently inert** for anyone who picked one — an unpriced turn costs 0, and a ceiling on 0 never trips. The fix is structural rather than six new rows. The generator's hand-curated allowlist is replaced by a **rule**: every model in LiteLLM's catalog whose id starts `gemini-`/`claude-`, whose `mode` is `chat`, that declares `supports_function_calling`, that is not past its `deprecation_date`, and that carries both a rate and a `max_input_tokens` — 12 entries become 31, and a model added upstream on a Monday needs no second edit here. `supports_function_calling` is decoded as `*bool` because an absent flag in that catalog does not mean `false`, and every near-miss is printed with the reason it was dropped, so the weekly PR shows what the rule *excluded* and not just what it kept. The picker then derives from that table (Gemini 3.x+, Claude 4.x+, minus date-pinned aliases and the Mythos tier's duplicate ids) and so cannot outrun it again; narrowing the picker narrows nothing else, since any id the provider accepts still runs via `--model` / `model.name` and still prices by longest-prefix match. The context-window map gains a **generated exact-match table** consulted ahead of the existing substring fallback, which corrects a case the substrings got wrong: `gemini-2.5-pro` was mapped to 2,000,000 — Gemini 1.5 Pro's window, carried forward — so the `0.85 × window` compaction trigger sat at 1.62× the real 1,048,576 limit and threshold-based compaction could never fire before the provider hard-failed the request. `pkg/modeltier` picks up the Mythos tier and Opus 5, which were classifying as unknown (no per-tier compaction threshold, and the small-tier-parent guard unable to reason about them). Four new invariant tests convert the next drift into a build failure rather than a support question: the two generated maps must have identical keys, every window must be inside a plausible range, every picker entry must be priced *and* classified *and* sized, and `taskclass.ModelForTier` must name the newest model in its line — the last one enforcing a policy that isn't derivable from LiteLLM (the catalog has no recency field) and that auto-promotion would get wrong in the worst way, by shipping an un-UAT'd frontier model to every operator on a regen Monday. Docs and examples move to the new defaults in the same pass, including the stale claim in the providers page that Claude pricing was absent from `usage.PriceFor` entirely.
- ci/pricing: **the weekly pricing regen actually opens its pull request now, and only when a rate has moved.** Four defects were stacked on `pricing-regen.yml`, and every one of them had to be fixed for the workflow to do anything. (1) The job authenticated with `GITHUB_TOKEN`, which the `go-steer` org forbids from creating pull requests — the 2026-08-10 run regenerated, verified and then died on `GitHub Actions is not permitted to create or approve pull requests`. A repository cannot override an org-level denial, so the job now mints a short-lived installation token for the `go-steer-bot` GitHub App from org-level `GOSTEER_BOT_CLIENT_ID` / `GOSTEER_BOT_PRIVATE_KEY` secrets. (2) Lifting that block alone would not have been enough: pushes made with `GITHUB_TOKEN` never trigger workflow runs — GitHub's loop guard — and `main` requires seven status checks, so the bot's PR would have opened with every check stuck at `Expected` and been unmergeable forever. An App token is a distinct identity and does trigger them, which is the reason to prefer it over merely relaxing the org policy. (3) `review-gate.yml` exempts the pricing bot by author login so its Go-touching PR isn't blocked for lacking an adversarial-review section, but the exemption was hardcoded to `github-actions[bot]`; switching identity would have turned a permanently-pending check into a hard-failing one. It is now an explicit two-name allowlist — deliberately not a `user.type == 'Bot'` test, which would silently enfranchise any third-party bot installed later. (4) Most consequentially, the drift test was `git diff --quiet pkg/pricing/builtin.go` *after* regenerating, and the generator stamps `time.Now()` onto every entry unconditionally — so it reported drift on any day the file had not been regenerated, whether or not LiteLLM had moved a single rate. It was testing the calendar. Left as-is behind the other three fixes it would have opened a no-op date-churn PR every Monday, training the reviewer to rubber-stamp the one diff that must be read carefully. The generator grows `--check`, which writes nothing and compares the rate-bearing content with the `UpdatedAt` stamps, the header's regeneration date and the header's `--source` provenance normalized away, plus gofmt's column padding collapsed so that one entry gaining a digit doesn't blame the neighbours it re-aligned; the report itemizes which models changed rather than dumping a whole-file diff. The verdict rides on **stdout** as `drift=true` / `drift=false` rather than the exit status, because `go run` collapses every non-zero child status to 1 — an exit-code convention would have made a failed LiteLLM fetch indistinguishable from a real price change, and all four callers invoke the generator through `go run`. The same false positive lived in the release guards (`release.yml`, `dev/release/cut-dev-tag.sh`, `dev/release/cut-ga-tag.sh`), where it was the reason a tag cut on any day but a regen day failed its pricing preflight; they use `--check` too, and as a side effect no longer dirty the working tree on their way past. Context: [#259](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/259) Slice B, whose automation half has never once run to completion.
- usage/pricing/models: **Anthropic cache-*write* tokens are now billed at their own premium rate**, where every one of them was charged at the base input rate. Anthropic reports three *disjoint* input buckets — `input_tokens` (the uncached remainder), `cache_read_input_tokens`, and `cache_creation_input_tokens` — and prices them differently: reads at 0.1× input, writes at **1.25×** on the 5-minute TTL (2× on the 1-hour one). The provider adapter folded all three into genai's `PromptTokenCount` and surfaced only reads as `CachedContentTokenCount`, so the tracker's `uncached = input − cached` silently absorbed the write bucket and priced it at 1×, understating each cache-warming turn by `cache_creation × input_rate × 0.25`. This was structural, not an oversight in one call site: `pricing.Rates` had no write-rate field to bill against and genai's `UsageMetadata` — modelled on Gemini's two buckets — has nowhere to put a third count. Both ends are now filled in. `Rates`/`Pricing`/`config.model.pricing` gain `cache_creation_input_per_mtok`, the LiteLLM refresh stops discarding the `cache_creation_input_token_cost` it already parsed, and the regenerated builtin table carries a write rate for all seven Claude rows (a test fails if any claude-* row's write rate isn't above its input rate, since an air-gapped daemon silently falling back to the old undercount is exactly how this shipped in the first place). The token count travels on `LLMResponse.CustomMetadata` under `cache_creation_input_tokens` rather than in a widened usage struct — that map is the one per-event field surviving ADK's persist round-trip, the same mechanism `pkg/eventlog` uses for `FinishReason`, which is what lets `usage.Rebuild` price a reloaded session identically to the live run; readers accept the `int64` stamped in-process and the `float64`/`json.Number` a JSON decode yields, and anything unrecognised reads as zero, degrading to the old understated number rather than to a bogus charge. Turn cost now has exactly one definition, `Pricing.CostUSDForTurn`, applied after a `TurnUsage.Clamped()` that enforces the buckets are disjoint and non-negative; the tracker-less fallbacks in `pkg/agent/autonomous` and `pkg/agent/subtask` route through it too, which incidentally fixes a pre-existing divergence where `SkipParentUsage` subtasks billed the *entire* prompt — cache reads included — at the base input rate. `GET /sessions/{sid}/usage` gains `input_tokens_cache_write` on both `overall`/`per_model` and each `per_turn` row (`omitempty`, so nothing changes on the wire for providers that don't bill writes per token — Gemini/Vertex charge cache *storage* per hour), and `input_tokens_uncached` now excludes it. The bucket is carried the whole way to every surface that shows a split, because a partial rollout is its own bug — an operator who sees `20,000 cached / 5,000 uncached` on a 25,000-token turn and is charged for something else has no way to tell which number lied. `/usage` renders a `/ N cache-write` clause on the totals, per-model and per-turn lines; the OTel token histogram gains a `cache_write` `token_type` (its absence made the metric's own buckets fail to sum to the total); `/pricing` names the write rate on the rate card; and the **internal** LLM calls get it too — compaction re-sends the entire transcript and is the single largest cache write in a session, so the tap that records it (`recordInternalLLMUsage`) now takes the sidecar rather than only the genai metadata. One adjacent data-loss fix rides along: `/pricing set <model> <in> <out>` replaced the whole manual entry, so bumping a base rate silently discarded a hand-edited `cached_input_per_mtok`/`cache_creation_input_per_mtok` on that model — it now updates in place and says which cache rates it kept. The meter was fixed ahead of the feature, deliberately: it lands first so the bill is already honest on Anthropic's *automatic* caching (first-party and Claude Platform on AWS; not Bedrock, not Vertex), and so the explicit prompt caching wired in the same release ([#714](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/714)) starts writing cache entries against numbers that can be trusted. Closes [#263](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/263).
- agentenv/config: the `.agents/env.yaml` drift report now counts **`*_env` config fields as references**. A bundle has two ways to consume an env var — `${env:NAME}` spliced into instruction text at load time, and a `*_env` config field handing the *name* to a component that resolves it late — but only the first flowed through the interpolator, and the drift check saw nothing else. So a var declared in the manifest and used solely by `alerts.targets[].url_env` was reported as `manifest declares "X" but nothing in the bundle references it`, which the live `kube-platform-native` deployment printed on every boot about the very webhook its alert target was reading. The two conventions are not interchangeable and neither is going away: `url_env` is re-read on each fire so a rotated Secret needs no restart, and the URL never enters the in-memory config that log and status surfaces echo — writing `"url": "${env:MY_WEBHOOK}"` instead doesn't work, since config is never interpolated and the validator rejects the unexpanded literal for having no scheme. New `(*config.Config).EnvRefs()` collects the names, `(*agentenv.Resolver).NoteConfigRefs` records them, and `cmd/core-agent` feeds one into the other before `ReportDrift`. They are kept in a **separate set** from the interpolation-seen names so the undeclared-reference warning can name the convention that actually made the reference — pointing an operator at a `${env:FOO}` that exists in no file, when `FOO` is a `bearer_env`, is a worse diagnostic than the one being fixed. This also turns on the half of the check that never existed: a `*_env` field naming a var the manifest doesn't declare now warns, where before it was silent. Discovery is reflective over JSON tags rather than a maintained field list — the [#759](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/759) rule that a list beside the thing it describes drifts on the first field someone adds — with a tag-inventory test that fails when the set of `*_env` fields changes, so a new env-name field tagged outside the convention gets caught in review rather than becoming a spurious warning nobody traces back. Warnings only; nothing about boot or resolution changed.
- attach/compose: **`/replan` now works on sessions the multi-session hub created**, where every one of them answered `501 capability not registered`. Only the daemon's own startup session could revoke a plan, because the hub deferred the `Replanner` closure as needing per-session threading it didn't yet have. That was survivable while nothing ran plan-first gating under the hub, and the kube-platform recipe now does: with `plan_mode: required`, a tenant could arm the gate by recording a plan and then have no way to revoke it — the daemon session's `/replan` correctly declines to touch a plan another agent recorded, which is [#747](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/747) working as designed and leaves the operator with nothing that works. The handler is revoked against that session's own sub-gate and scoped to `{agent, session}`, which matters more here than in single-session mode: `.agents/plans/` is process-global while the gate is per-session, so an owner-blind revoke would archive whichever tenant planned most recently. The closure body moved to the new `attachadapter.ReplanHandler` so both hosts run the same code rather than the hub carrying a second copy of the message logic [#747](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/747) established. `RefreshPricer`, `PricingSetter` and `Reloader` stay unwired — those deferrals are about scope (a daemon-wide config write, an unresolved MCP-restart story), not threading. Closes [#763](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/763).
- tools/alert: an alert target whose webhook URL or token comes from an **unset environment variable is now dropped at startup**, and a build where no target survives registers no `alert` tool at all. Both the URL (`url_env`) and the auth material (`auth.bearer_env`, `auth.basic_env_user`/`basic_env_pass`) were resolved only at call time, on the reasoning that a rotated Secret should be picked up without a restart. The rotation argument holds; the registration consequence did not. A process's environment is fixed at exec time, so "unset now" already means "unset for this process's whole life" — a Secret edit needs a pod restart either way — and registering the target anyway put an escalation path in the model's schema, and in the operator's `AGENTS.md`, that could only fail. In a live 2026-08-14 run it failed exactly where it costs the most: the agent worked an incident to `UNRESOLVED`, called `alert` to page the on-call, and reported *"the escalation webhook failed to deliver (`PLATFORM_AGENT_ALERT_WEBHOOK` is unset in this deployment), so nobody has been paged — this report is the only hand-off."* The dropped target is gone from the tool description the model reads and from the routing table (firing it by name returns `unknown target`, not a silent no-op), and `cmd/core-agent` prints one stderr line per drop naming the target and the variable, plus a louder line when nothing is left. Three deliberate non-choices: the boot is **not** failed, because alerting is optional and a daemon that can still triage beats one that won't start; the call path still re-resolves both URL and auth, so rotation keeps working and a variable that disappears mid-process fails closed rather than POSTing to an empty URL or going out unauthenticated; and recipe validation (`examples/internal/recipecheck`) deliberately ignores the rule, since whether a Secret is mounted is a property of the deployment, not of the recipe. Note that a recipe's *prose* can't be filtered this way — an `AGENTS.md` that says "escalate via `alert`" survives the tool's absence, so the `gke-troubleshoot-agent` skill now carries an explicit "if `alert` isn't in your tool list, say `Escalation: not sent (no alert target configured)`" fallback. Same rule as the description cross-references below, one layer down: never name a capability the deployment doesn't have.
- tools: **descriptions name only the tools this build registered.** A model-facing description that cross-references a tool that was never registered asserts a capability the model doesn't have, and the model spends a turn discovering that. On the distroless daemon — no shell, so `bash` and every shell-adjacent tool is disabled — six built-ins still advertised themselves as "PREFERRED over `bash cat`" / "`bash rm`" / "`bash stat`" / "`bash grep`" / "`bash curl`" / "`bash sleep`"; `bash` itself redirected code investigation to `read_file` / `grep` / `glob` / `list_dir` whether or not those were built; `record_plan` under `plan_mode: required` instructed "call this BEFORE any `write_file` / `edit_file` / `delete_file` / `bash` call", describing a constraint on nothing; and `spawn_agent`'s `tools` parameter carried a fixed example list (`… glob, grep, bash, todo …`) whose names `resolveTools` rejects with `ErrUnknownTool`. The gate already carried this knowledge for one case (`SetNativeSearchTools`, [#158](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/158)); this generalizes it as `Gate.SetRegisteredTools` / `HasTool`, fed by `tools.Build` from the `specs` table itself so the conditionally-registered tools (`fetch_url`, `alert`, `record_plan`, `sciontool_status`) are reported by what actually happened rather than by the toggle. Every rewritten description keeps its substance in the base sentence — only the comparison is conditional — and `spawn_agent`'s `tools` list is now rendered from the manager's real catalog (built-ins, MCP tools and skills alike, minus the auto-wired names), so a name the model reads there is a name the spawn will accept. Nil map and nil receiver both mean "assume registered", so an embedder wiring tools by hand keeps today's text verbatim. Scope: `tools.Build`'s own catalog — `spawn_agent` and MCP tools are wired elsewhere, so `HasTool` can't tell "absent" from "unknown" for them and references to them are always kept.
- multi-session: daemon shutdown now **joins** the per-session wake loops before closing the eventlog they read. `buildSession` started each loop with a bare `go runner.WakeLoop(...)` and kept no handle, so cancelling the daemon context only ever *signalled* them — and a signalled loop can still be mid-query against the eventlog when the handle goes away. The race detector found it in a test teardown, where `t.Cleanup`'s LIFO ordering ran `cancel()` before `h.Close()` and the cancel was assumed to be a join (`TestAutoContinueBootScan_BreakerAndSingleRetry`, [#751](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/751)). Production had the same ordering and worse: `defer handle.Close()` is registered ~200 lines before the loops exist, while the outermost `defer stop()` that cancels the daemon context is registered first and therefore runs *last* — so at Close time the loops had not been signalled at all, and the only thing standing between an operator and a use-after-close on the session DB was the process exiting quickly enough. The daemon now derives its own cancel for the session factory and drains through a new `compose.WakeLoopGroup` (`add` before the `go`, so a `WaitFor` racing session construction can't read an empty group as a completed drain), bounded at 5s and **reported** on the way out rather than swallowed — a loop still running past the window prints a line naming it, because the alternative symptom is a SQLite error on a closing connection with nothing attached to explain it. The join is deliberately not wired into the **eviction** path: the registry's sweep runs across every tenant, and blocking it on one session's in-flight turn is the trade the existing `bgClose` comment already rejects. Eviction signals; shutdown joins. The field is optional (`SessionFactoryDeps.WakeLoops`, nil = pre-fix behavior), so library callers that build their own teardown are unaffected. Fixes [#751](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/751).
- plan-first: `record_plan` now **reports what it actually did** — the gate it armed, the author of the artifact, and, through `/replan`, whose plan gets revoked. Three untruths, all found in the 2026-08-14 GKE UAT, all the same shape: a surface stating a property the runtime does not hold. (1) The result said "Mutating tools are now unblocked for this session" unconditionally. In that recipe `bash`, `write_file`, `edit_file` and `delete_file` were all disabled, so the sentence named an empty set — while saying nothing about the surface the plan really unblocked, the whole `gke` MCP namespace, which is deliberately not plan-exempt. It also said it in **advisory** mode, where nothing was ever blocked and nothing becomes callable, which is the [#215](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/215) description-split bug reappearing one surface over: a model that believes a gate exists behaves as though it does, and stalls for an approval nobody will send. The message now names the tools this build gates, drawn from what the host registered (`tools.Build`, each `GateToolset` namespace, `call_peer` under its operator-chosen name) via new `permissions.Gate.RegisterPlanGatedTools` / `PlanGatedTools`. That set is three-valued on purpose, mirroring `SetNativeSearchTools` ([#158](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/158)): a library caller that wires tools by hand registers nothing and gets prose that declines to enumerate, because inventing "nothing is gated" for it would be the same unenforceable claim pointed the other way. (2) Plan artifacts were anonymous. One `.agents/plans/` directory is shared by a parent and its declarative subagents — the UAT had both write into it in one incident — and in multi-session mode the gate flag is per-session while the directory is process-global, so concurrent tenants interleave into one sequence. Every artifact now opens with `plan:` / `agent:` / `session:` frontmatter, with keys omitted rather than emitted empty so "no attribution" is distinguishable from "recorded as empty". (3) `/replan` archived max-sequence, so once a subagent had recorded `plan-2` the operator's `/replan` revoked the *subagent's* plan and left the parent's active — the exact inversion of what the command is for. It is now scoped to the issuing agent and session (`tools.RevokePlanBy`, plus `PlanOwner` / `PlanInfo` / `ActivePlans`); a newer plan belonging to someone else is named in the response and left alone, a directory with no attribution anywhere still falls back to newest-wins so pre-v2.9 plans behave as before, and the gate flag clears either way so the safety contract holds even when nothing matched. `tools.RevokeLatestPlan` keeps its signature and its semantics as the zero-owner spelling. Not fixed here: `spawn_agent` is described as plan-gated in `--plan-mode`'s help and in the design doc, but nothing routes it through the gate — so it does not appear in the reported set, and the message reports the runtime rather than the intention. Fixes [#747](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/747).
- background subagents: a **refused spawn no longer reports like a successful one**. `spawn_agent`, `stop_agent` and `spawn_remote_agent` return their failures as the tool's *result* rather than as Go errors, so the model reads the refusal in conversation context and adapts — that half is deliberate and unchanged. The half that wasn't intended is that the model was the only reader who could tell: a refusal came back in the same `{name, branch, status}` shape as a launch, with the reason inside a status string nothing downstream parses. In the 2026-08-14 GKE UAT the [#742](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/742) self-spawn guard fired correctly and the TUI drew it as `↳ branch, name, status` with no `✗` and no error styling — indistinguishable from a spawn that worked, and the operator watching reported it as a subagent that had started. Confirming otherwise meant reading the subagent's branch events and noticing `branch: ""`. The refusal text now also populates the result's `error` field — ADK's reserved key for a failed call — so every consumer that already knows how to render, trace or count a failure does: both TUIs draw the error row with the reason (the same affordance a `tool not found` gets), the flow marks the tool span failed, and the watchdog's `tool-failure-streak` signal counts the call instead of scoring it a success, which is what a model retrying a refused spawn in a loop needs. `status` keeps its `error: ...` prose verbatim, so nothing the model sees changes. A launch that happened carries no `error` key; the field is about the *call*, not the run's outcome — a synchronous spawn whose subagent later fails still reports that through `stop_reason`. Fixes [#746](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/746).
- declarative subagents: omitting `tools:` no longer hands a subagent the **spawn tools**. Inheritance grants the parent's built-in registry minus `spawn_agent` and `stop_agent`; a spec that names them in `tools:` still gets them, which is how a deliberate orchestrator subagent is written, and the subagent's startup line reports `spawn=withheld (…; list them in tools: to grant)` whenever the carve-out fires. Omitting `tools:` is how an operator says "give me the parent's hardening" — it should not also say "give me its authority to build a fleet", and the sibling ad-hoc path had already made exactly this call: the per-session manager's catalog is built with the spawn tools stripped, so a subagent can't pick one out of the catalog either. Which of the two paths assembled the tool list shouldn't decide whether a subagent can delegate. The gate argument that makes inheritance safe doesn't cover this case, because delegation isn't a privilege the gate scores: a subagent calling `spawn_agent` does nothing the gate would refuse, it starts another agent. In the 2026-08-14 GKE UAT the inheriting `cluster` specialist tried to delegate its own investigation to a second `cluster`; [#742](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/742)'s lineage guard refused that call, but only because it was a *self*-spawn — with two specialists on the roster the same reflex fans out unbounded. New exported `background.SpawnToolNames` / `SpawnAgentToolName` / `StopAgentToolName` for callers that need to filter a tool slice rather than build one. Fixes [#748](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/748).
- background subagents: a subagent can no longer **spawn itself**. `spawn_agent` refuses a reference to any configured subagent already running as an ancestor of the call, with a tool error naming it so the model reroutes onto doing the work rather than retrying blindly. The depth cap (`WithSubagentMaxDepth` / `defaultMaxDepth`, both 2) was doing its job and was never the constraint that was needed: in the 2026-08-13 GKE UAT the `cluster` subagent spawned `cluster-2` with a byte-identical goal at **depth 1**, and two agents then investigated the same incident concurrently, both billing the parent session. Self-recursion sits inside any sensible depth cap by definition, so no value of the cap could have caught it, and lowering the default would break legitimate two-level fan-out instead. The match is on the **declared** name — the template or predefined spec — not the per-instance name, since instances are `<spec>-<n>` and comparing those would catch nothing; a new `Spec.Ref` carries the declaration past the point where the caller overwrites `Spec.Name` with the instance name. The synchronous half of the stack is recorded too: a declarative subagent is reachable both as a parent tool call and by reference under one name ([#626](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/626)), so a subagent invoked as a tool would otherwise have been able to spawn itself asynchronously. Duplicate-*goal* suppression is deliberately not attempted — goal similarity is fuzzy, and the structural guard covers the observed failure at zero false-positive risk. Fixes [#732](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/732).
- attach/background subagents: `GET /sessions/{sid}/subagents` no longer advertises a **synchronous subagent tool the session does not have**. `Manager.Catalog` hardcoded `modes: ["sync","async"]` for every declarative template, on every manager, on the assumption that a declarative subagent is always both a parent tool and spawn-by-reference. That holds for the daemon's primary agent and for nothing else: `compose.ReproduceAgent` wires a daemon-created session with the background manager but never `agent.WithSubagents`, so a session created through `POST /sessions` can reach a declarative subagent asynchronously only. In the 2026-08-13 GKE UAT the two operator surfaces disagreed — `/subagents` reported `modes:["sync","async"]` for `cluster` on a session whose `/tools` listing carried no `cluster` entry and whose model was never offered one, the same two-surfaces-disagreeing shape `TestSessionBackgroundRecipe_BindsSpawnToolsToTheSessionManager` exists to prevent, in a place nobody was checking. `Catalog` now derives the modes from the attached parent's `SubagentNames()` — the set `agent.WithSubagents` actually wired — so `"sync"` is a claim about *this* session's tool surface rather than a property of the template, and it cannot drift from what the model was offered. With no parent attached yet the answer is async-only, the under-claiming direction. Predefined catalog specs are unchanged (always async-only), and the response shape is otherwise untouched. This makes the asymmetry **visible; it does not close it** — a `POST /sessions` session still has no synchronous subagent tool, and a detached `spawn_agent { wait: true }` result still reaches its parent only when the parent happens to take another turn. Those two remain open ([#741](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/741)).
- autonomous: `WithMaxCost` is now enforced **inside** a turn, and a subagent's `Budgets.MaxCost` binds even when the parent keeps no usage tracker. Two independent holes let a bounded delegation spend without a bound. The first: every budget was checked between turns, but a turn is an unbounded tool loop — the [#144](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/144) runaway shape never reaches a turn boundary, so the spend cap sat idle through exactly the spend it exists to cap, and the check finally ran to stop a run it had already paid for in full. Cost is now compared against the bound after each committed model call, on the driver's own accumulator, which is baselined at the start of the run by construction; a trip stops draining the turn and ends the run with `max_cost_exceeded` carrying the text the model had produced, since [#691](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/691) already routes a budget stop's findings to the parent. One exception, taken at most once per turn: the check fires on a model *response* and the tool it requested runs after it, so if the call that crosses the bound is the model handing back its result, cutting the turn there would discard the answer and report a budget stop instead — [#728](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/728)'s failure arriving through a different door. The loop takes one more step in that case and stops the instant the done signal lands, so the grace buys the tool its execution and never buys the model another call. Enforcing in the driver rather than arming the subagent `Agent`'s own ceiling is deliberate: a spawned subagent has no tracker of its own (the driver appends to the *parent's*), and `usage.Tracker` has no per-source attribution, so a "spawn-time baseline" on the shared tracker would charge an async subagent for the parent's concurrent spend and halt it for someone else's tokens. The second hole: pricing rode along with `WithTracker`, so a parent with no tracker handed its subagents a `MaxCost` the driver could never evaluate — every turn priced out at exactly `$0.00` and the budget was decoration, which is worse than no budget because the caller believes the delegation is bounded. Pricing is now supplied unconditionally and the tracker only when there is one. The other caps are unchanged and still evaluated between turns: turns and wallclock are bounded by `max_steps` and `PerTurnTimeout` respectively, and a token cap tripping mid-turn would change behavior for existing consumers without closing a spend hole. Fixes [#729](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/729).
- background subagents: **returning a result is now one gesture, under every name a model reaches for.** A spawned subagent's namespace had accumulated three near-synonymous completion tools of which exactly one worked: `report_done` ended the run; `report_completed` pushed a "completed" alert and returned `ok` **without** ending it — its own description told the model to "call `report_done` separately to actually terminate the autonomous loop" — and `mark_task_done`, a name models reach for from training, was never registered on subagents at all. Both wrong choices land in the same place: the tool acks, the driver hands the model `continue`, and the run grinds to its turn cap with `status: "deferred"` while the answer it already had scrolls out of the returned value. That is the shape of the 2026-08-13 GKE run in which a subagent produced a correct root-cause analysis on turn one and the parent received "standing by in a healthy, inactive state". All three names, plus a new primary `return_result`, are now aliases of a single tool that delivers the payload **and** ends the run. Two details are load-bearing rather than incidental: the aliases accept the legacy `report_done(state, detail)` argument shape as well as `{result}`, because ADK validates function arguments with `additionalProperties: false` — an alias that rejected the exact call shape it exists to catch would be worse than no alias — and an **empty** result is refused with a corrective ack instead of terminating, since a return that ends the run with nothing in hand is the failure the tool exists to prevent, and the delegating agent is no worse off for one more turn. `report_alert` stays a separate tool on purpose: a mid-run progress note is a different message from a return, and folding them together would let a status update end a delegation. The framing also moves **out of the tool description and into the instruction** (`## Returning your result`), because the description is only read by a model that goes looking for that tool — while on a natural stop, a budget cap or a watchdog halt it is the subagent's *last assistant text* that reaches the parent, and a subagent that never learned its output was a return value writes a closing pleasantry there. Both delegation paths install it from one renderer (new `agent.SubagentReturnContract`), so a declarative subagent reached as a parent **tool** and the same subagent reached through `spawn_agent` are told the same thing — with the sync rendering omitting the tool sentence, because that path registers no return tool and instructing a model to call one would be the unenforced-claim pattern in miniature. The driver change is opt-in (`autonomous.WithReturnTool`); a consumer that doesn't set it keeps the historical `report_done(state, detail)` lifecycle tool, and `WithDoneToolName` / `WithDoneToolDescription` behave as before on both paths. See `docs/subagent-return-contract-design.md`. Fixes [#727](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/727) and [#728](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/728).
- background subagents: a **terminal alert** now carries the subagent's findings, not just its status line. [#667](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/667) made a synchronous `spawn_agent { wait: true }` return the subagent's actual output, but only on the path where the wait completes — once the sync cap expires, `abandonWait` releases the deliver-once claim and delivery moves to the async terminal alert, which rendered `DoneDetail` alone. The fix was therefore lost exactly when the subagent had run long enough to be worth waiting for. In the [#622](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/622) UAT a subagent ran 6m21s past a 5m cap, and all of its diagnosis that reached the parent was "successfully diagnosed the FailedMount ... and provided the proposed manifest patch" — with no patch, because the patch was in the last assistant text nothing forwarded. The parent redid the entire investigation: 91 turns, 2.69M input tokens, $1.31. The alert is now rendered by one helper that is the async twin of the sync path's `completionResult`, appending the last assistant text under the same `final_text` label the tool result uses, so the two surfaces carry the same content in different shapes rather than drifting. Two adjacent gaps close with it: a **deferred** (budget-capped) or **failed** subagent never calls `report_done` at all, so its last assistant text was the only record of what it found and the alert was throwing it away in favor of a bare `stopped: max_turns_exceeded`; both now keep the outcome line *and* the findings. A `stopped` alert stays terse by decision — the parent asked for the stop, and a cancelled mid-thought is not a finding. Fixes [#691](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/691).
- internal/vertexcache: a **transient** `Caches.Create` failure no longer disables context caching for the daemon's lifetime. [#370](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/370) split Create failures into two classes — context cancellation retried, everything else permanently sticky — and put an IAM error in the permanent class, which is wrong for the most common way a fresh deployment fails: a pod that starts before its Workload Identity binding has propagated gets `403 PERMISSION_DENIED` on its first turn, is correctly configured within about two minutes, and then runs uncached forever anyway. In the [#707](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/707) GKE run one session paid full input price for all 365K of its billed input tokens on a project where caching had been working the day before. A non-context Create failure is now retried on a bounded backoff (15s, 30s, 1m, 2m, 4m — six attempts spanning roughly eight minutes), and only after that budget is spent does the manager go sticky-failed. Retries are **demand-driven**: the gemini provider already calls `Init` on every non-cached model call, so the schedule is a floor on retry spacing rather than a timer, and an idle daemon issues no RPCs at all. The bound is what preserves the original intent — a genuinely misconfigured project stops being hammered and still gets a loud, greppable give-up line (`Caches.Create failed 6 times (giving up; ...)`), while each retry logs its attempt number and next-eligible delay so an operator watching a rollout can tell "waiting on IAM" from "fix your config". Fixes [#707](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/707).
- attach: `GET /sessions/.../agents/{name}/events` now resolves the name an operator **declared**, and says so when it can't resolve anything. The background manager mints instance names as `<spec>-<n>`, so a subagent declared `cluster` writes its turns on branch `bg.cluster-1` — which matched neither `bg.cluster` (not equal) nor `bg.cluster.%` (an instance counter is not a dot segment), and the endpoint answered a clean `200` with zero events, indistinguishable from "it ran and did nothing". Name resolution now consults the log's own distinct branch labels (new optional `eventlog.BranchLister` extension: `SELECT DISTINCT branch`, no event hydration) and adds the instance-suffixed spellings it finds to the query; only a `-<digits>` suffix counts, so a separate subagent named `cluster-probe` is not folded into a query for `cluster`. Because absence is now *observed* rather than inferred from the in-memory manager, an unresolvable name is a `404` carrying `available` — every name that would have resolved, from the log plus the live and configured rosters. A name in either roster still answers `200` with an empty list, as does a `Stream` that can't enumerate its branches, so the `404` only ever means "looked, and it isn't here". Fixes [#694](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/694).
- agent: a **resumed** session no longer false-trips its *per-turn* cost ceiling on the first turn. On resume the usage tracker is rebuilt with the whole session's prior spend before the agent is constructed (`pkg/compose/multi_session.go`), but the turn-start baseline began at zero — so the first turn's "delta" was the entire history, and any session whose accumulated spend already exceeded `max_turn_cost_usd` halted before it had cost a cent. The per-turn check now requires a baseline captured by a turn this process actually ran. The per-session check is untouched: it reads the accumulator directly, which is exactly what should carry across a resume. Surfaced while implementing [#643](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/643).
- background subagents: a synchronous delegation (`spawn_agent { wait: true }`) now returns the subagent's **work**, not a status line about it. Three fixes compose: (1) spawned subagents get a purpose-built `report_done` description that says the report is the deliverable handed back to the delegating agent and that a summary of what you did forces the parent to redo it — the driver's stock prose asked for "a one-sentence detail", which is exactly what produced content-free reports like "diagnosed the issue and proposed a patch" in the kube-platform-agent UAT; (2) the tool result gains a secondary `final_text` channel carrying the subagent's last assistant text when it says something the completion report doesn't (`output` keeps its report-preferred semantics — `final_text` is only the *last* turn's text and is often a closing pleasantry, so it supplements rather than replaces); (3) `autonomous.Run` now collects consolidated non-partial response text, so `RunResult.FinalText` is no longer always empty for a non-streaming model — previously only streaming partials were accumulated, which silently emptied `FinalText` (and the `ai.LastReport` fallback and `examples/autonomous*` output) for any non-streaming provider. Fixes [#641](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/641).
- background subagents: a successful `wait: true` delegation no longer double-delivers. `awaitResult` takes a sync claim on the handle *before* it blocks, and the completion goroutine consumes that claim in place of pushing its `[Background reports]` alert, so the result arrives exactly once — inline, as the tool result. Exactly one side wins the race by construction: a waiter that times out or is canceled releases the claim and the alert takes the normal async path, so a result is never dropped. Fire-and-continue spawns are unchanged. One window stays open deliberately: a subagent that reaches a terminal state before the waiter claims has already passed the suppression check, so the (already-queued) alert is still delivered — a duplicate, never a loss. Fixes [#646](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/646).

#### Documentation
- docs: staleness + over-claiming sweep over the four docs the 2026-08-12 tri-team assessment flagged as describing a runtime that doesn't exist. `k8s-event-agent-design.md` opened by telling a reader to build `cmd/k8s-event-watcher/` against `k8s.io/client-go` — a directory and a dependency this repo hasn't had since the 2026-07-24 k8s-lookout extraction — so it now carries a *Moved* banner naming the new home and image (`ghcr.io/go-steer/lookout`), the design body is kept and labeled historical, and the two "Resolved: in-tree monorepo" / "prompt-pattern is enough" decisions are re-resolved against what happened (separate repo; `wait_and_verify` shipped, because the prompt-pattern's fallback was `bash sleep` in a recipe that disables `bash`, making the verify step inexpressible rather than merely flaky). `alert-tool-design.md` still read "proposed, awaiting approval, v2.7 candidate" for a tool that shipped in v2.9 and specified four templates when the binary implements one — its status is now *partially shipped*, with the `slack`/`discord`/`pagerduty_events_v2` sections marked as a specification for unwritten code rather than a description of behavior. `north-star.md` advertised the four-tool `spawn`/`list`/`check`/`report` subagent surface that [#625](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/625) folded to two, and still said main was on `v2.8.0-dev`. `kube-agents-platform-fit.md`'s headline "80% reusable today" now states what it measures — architectural fit, nothing benchmarked or deployed — since the peer fabric it leans on is an in-memory map with no persistence and `call_peer` does not exist. Also corrects `DEMO.md`'s Scene 7, which credited v2.7 with three things it didn't ship (the `alert` tool — v2.9; OAuth-authenticated MCP servers — v1.5.0; cron-driven proactive operations and LLM-authored diagnostic tools — designed, unshipped), and now names v2.9's `wait_and_verify` + `alert` as what the demo actually runs on. Closes [#650](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/650).
- examples: the `gke-troubleshoot-agent` recipe is **executable again** — every action it names is now one the daemon can actually take. The shipped `k8s-triage` skill and its 11 reason references were written as kubectl runbooks ("`kubectl rollout undo`", "`kubectl delete pod`", "`sleep 120`, then re-check") for a distroless daemon whose config sets `tools.disable: ["bash"]` and whose only MCP server exposes no pod-level mutation verb at all: the recipe promised a diagnose→fix→verify loop and shipped no way to fix or to wait. A model handed that content either errors on a tool it doesn't have or — the failure this milestone is about — reports `Status: RESOLVED` for a rollback it never performed. Rather than invent a mutation surface the wired server does not serve, the recipe is re-scoped to what it can prove: **diagnose → verify → propose → escalate**, made true by construction at four layers rather than asserted in a persona. `mcp.json` points at `container.googleapis.com/mcp/read-only` with the read-only OAuth scope (the mutating verbs are never in the catalog); `tools.disable` drops `write_file`/`edit_file`/`delete_file`/`fetch_url` alongside `bash`; `scripts/setup-wif.sh` binds `roles/container.viewer` instead of `roles/container.admin`; and every reference's old "common fixes" table became `| Evidence | Proposed change | Verify |`, which the router writes into the incident summary for a human to apply. The convergence check is now a real `wait_and_verify` call against a `gke_*` read tool (registered through the new `tools.wait_and_verify.poll_allow`, since MCP tools never self-classify as read-only), and `verified: true` is the *only* thing the skill accepts as grounds for `RESOLVED` — a `wait_and_verify` that times out yields `UNRESOLVED` plus a proposal, which is a finding, not a failure ([#639](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/639) through the recipe door). Escalation stops being a documented TODO and becomes wiring: an `oncall` alert target on `ONCALL_WEBHOOK_URL` (rate-limited `10/min`, `generic` template — the only one implemented), fed by an optional `core-agent-alerts` Secret so the pod still boots without one. `record_plan` replaces the `write_file` the old content used for plan artifacts, which the new `require_plan_artifact: true` gate makes mandatory before *any* `gke` call, read-only ones included. A new `examples/gke-troubleshoot-agent/recipe_test.go` pins all of it — loader validation, the read-only endpoint and scope, poll_allow ↔ reference agreement in both directions, the alert target, the manifest's Secret wiring, the IAM grant, and a content scan that fails on any `kubectl`/`gcloud`/`sleep`/mutating-`gke_` verb in the skill tree — so the docs and the enforcement can't drift apart again. DEMO.md's Scene 3 now ends with the operator applying the agent's proposal, and its "v2.7 shipped Slack/PagerDuty escalation" line is corrected to the `generic` webhook that actually shipped. Closes [#644](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/644).
- docs: `poll_allow` examples across `docs/wait-and-verify-design.md`, the configuration reference, the tools concept page, and `pkg/config`'s field comment named MCP tools with a **double** underscore (`gke__get_pod`). `pkg/mcp/namespace.go` joins server and tool with a single one, so every documented example was a name no server exposes — and because an unmatched `poll_allow` entry is not a config error, copying it yields a `wait_and_verify` that is silently refused at call time. Corrected, with the separator now stated explicitly in the reference. Follow-up to [#648](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/648).
- examples: new `bouncer-preflight` — the generator + checker pair of [gke-demos/bouncer](https://github.com/gke-demos/bouncer) (a Python `google-adk` system that derives verified single-slice TPU preflight smoke tests from completed GKE production workloads) ported onto core-agent as a **library consumer**, with the upstream prompts copied verbatim and `pkg/`-only imports. It is the worked answer to "what does a foreign agent framework need that a config recipe can't give it", and each of the four answers is a pattern worth copying: model-authored shell contained by registering bouncer's `bwrap` + `sudo -u agent-runner` jail as the **only** shell (the built-in `bash` is never wired in, and the jail's flags are asserted individually — a silently dropped `--unshare-user` fails a test, not a pentest); **structured output inverted into a tool**, since core-agent has no `output_schema` — the checker's terminal *action* is `report_verdict(success, details)`, whose Go signature is the schema, fail-closed when never called (replacing upstream's `"success: True"` stdout grep); a **synchronous typed hand-off** between two agents in one process, replacing `subprocess.Popen(["adk","run","checker"])` so a transport failure is a tool error rather than a verdict about the manifest; and the upstream `BaseApiClient.async_request` retry monkeypatch as an ordinary `adkmodel.LLM` decorator. Runs hermetically end-to-end — two scripted transcripts plus a fake `kubectl`, no credentials, no cluster — and a test fails the build if a prompt names a tool the port doesn't register.
- examples: the `kube-platform-agent` recipe now loads its workspace `AGENTS.md` and all 18 skills through a **content root** (`content_roots: ["../upstream"]`) instead of copying the skill tree into `.agents/skills/` — the 18 skill dirs moved into the faithful `upstream/` snapshot, so there is no longer a copy to drift from the vendored source. Replacing the recipe's single `content_roots` entry with a real, unmodified kube-agents checkout is the documented "live" mode, delivering the "run kube-agents with core-agent, unmodified" proof for [#594](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/594) (the README notes that `--agents-content-dir` *layers after* the config list, so it adds a root rather than switching runtimes). `SOUL.md` stays `@include`d by the project-root `AGENTS.md` (a content root auto-assembles only the workspace `AGENTS.md`, and upstream splits its persona across `SOUL.md`/`AGENTS.md`/`CAPABILITIES.md`); the loader test now asserts the content-root load, the *absence* of a copied skill tree, and a live external-checkout fixture. The cluster subagent initially stayed `skills: []`; [#621](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/621) later gave it its own **per-subagent content root** (`root: ../cluster`) so it loads its six GKE domain-diagnostic skills from a self-contained `cluster/` tree independent of the platform parent (see the enrichment-RBAC / cluster-root entry below). Part of epic [#589](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/589).
- examples: the `kube-platform-agent` recipe gains a `deploy/` kustomize tree (base + `overlays/example` + `overlays/initcontainer-copy`) that runs the hub daemon and the [lookout](https://github.com/go-steer/k8s-lookout) event watcher in a GKE cluster, mirroring the `gke-troubleshoot-agent` deploy shape (namespace, KSA + Workload Identity, RBAC, session-db PVC, `users.json` initContainer, `Service`). Because the recipe's content (~1.3 MiB of workspace + 18 skills + governance) is too large for a ConfigMap, it ships as an **OCI image volume** built `FROM scratch` (`deploy/content.Dockerfile`), keeping recipe content and the `core-agent` brain image on independent lifecycles; a companion `overlays/initcontainer-copy` delivers the *same* content image (Chainguard-busybox flavor) via an initContainer `cp` into a shared `emptyDir` for clusters below the image-volume floor (k8s < 1.33 / GKE < 1.35). The watcher authenticates as its own `sa:lookout-watch` proxy identity while asserting the admin owner, and a guard test pins that auth wiring to `config.hub.json` so a drift in either identity fails the build rather than 403-ing at runtime. Establishes the general content-distribution pattern documented in [`docs/agent-content-distribution-design.md`](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/blob/main/docs/agent-content-distribution-design.md) ([#611](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/611)). Part of epic [#589](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/589).
- examples: the `kube-platform-agent` recipe now ships lookout's **enrichment-complete** watcher RBAC and tracks lookout's current release, `v0.18.0`. The watcher previously ran a minimal ClusterRole (events + `pods:get`), which starved lookout's enrichment — every incident inject carried `enrichment_error stage=resolve` instead of a warm correlated bundle. It now vendors lookout's read-only (`get`/`list`/`watch`) enrichment ClusterRole (`deploy/base/12-clusterrole-watcher.yaml`, object name suffixed `-kube-platform` so it coexists with a plain lookout install), adds a kube-system-scoped capacity `Role`/`RoleBinding` (`14`/`15`) for the `cluster-autoscaler-status` ConfigMap and a default-deny `NetworkPolicy` (`16`), and replaces the base `namespace:` shorthand with a builtin `NamespaceTransformer` (`unsetOnly: true`) so the capacity objects keep their explicit `kube-system` namespace instead of being clobbered. v0.14.0 remains the capability floor (per-resource-`Forbidden` tolerance, [k8s-lookout#192](https://github.com/go-steer/k8s-lookout/issues/192)); the recipe pins `v0.18.0`, and the naming it uses came from `v0.17.0`, which **retired the `k8s-event-watcher` transition naming** (lookout#205/#206) — every watcher resource here (ServiceAccount, Deployment, ClusterRole(Binding), Role(Binding), NetworkPolicy, the `lookout-watch-token` Secret) and the daemon's `sa:lookout-watch` proxy identity are renamed to match. The declarative `cluster` subagent moves to a **per-subagent content root** (`root: ../cluster`, [#619](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/619)): its persona, six GKE domain-diagnostic skills, and read-only MCP load from a self-contained `cluster/` tree independent of the platform parent (which returns to its 18 platform skills), decoupling the earlier constraint where the subagent's skills had to be a vendored subset of the parent's `.agents/skills/`. The daemon Deployment now also runs with `--watchdog=enforce` ([#623](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/623)): a headless, auto-continue daemon has nobody tailing the default `warn`-mode logs, so a runaway tool loop must trip a `kind=watchdog` turn-error and refuse new turns until reset rather than silently re-driving the interrupted turn until `max_steps` and burning Vertex tokens. Closes [#618](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/618) and [#621](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/621); part of epic [#589](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/589).
- examples: the `kube-platform-agent` recipe is **frozen as a portability case study** and is no longer the recommended starting point for a new agent ([#704](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/704)). It set out to prove the v2 loader can run an *unmodified* third-party (Hermes) agent snapshot, and it did: content roots, `@include`, per-subagent content roots, the skills loader, declarative subagents, the read-only MCP surface, plan-first, the attach hub, the OCI content image, and the lookout integration all work against foreign content. Live GKE UAT produced the narrower result the freeze records — **the mechanics port, identity does not.** The imported persona is a Hermes *kanban worker* ("accept a task → loop until done → file a completion report → exit"), that identity travels with the markdown, and reconciling it from a core-agent overlay fails at the moments that matter because the overlaid content speaks last: the agent confabulated verification it had never run, forced a general question into a canned completion report, and looped where "done" was unreachable. The sharpest instance is fixed here rather than left broken ([#703](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/703)) — `cluster/AGENTS.md`'s overlay correctly tells the subagent to return its RCA in its reply and **lost** to Step 5 of `cluster/skills/gke-workload-troubleshooting/SKILL.md`, which told it to withhold the RCA and hand off through a kanban card that no tool in this runtime provides, because skills load at the point of use and therefore speak last. That one step is rewritten to make the report the deliverable (the single deviation from the six otherwise-unmodified cluster skills, recorded in `upstream/PROVENANCE.md`), the overlay now claims precedence over skill-level instructions, and a guard test fails the build if a `cluster/skills/` step reaches for a kanban tool again. `upstream/` and `cluster/SOUL.md` are left untouched — they are the evidence. The recipe stays deployable and CI-covered; `deploy/` is persona-independent and remains the reference GKE deployment shape.

#### Other (Cleanup)
- ci: **every job now runs the Go we ship**, which closes the source-side half of [#736](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/736). That fix made the *published image* build on `go.mod`'s `toolchain` directive and verified the artifact before pushing it, but left the other end alone: CI asked `setup-go` for a floating `'1.26'` with `check-latest: true` — deliberately the newest patch release, which is not the one we pin. Source-mode `govulncheck` reports stdlib advisories against the toolchain running it, so the `vuln` job was scanning a standard library nobody downloads, and a CVE fixed in a patch newer than our pin read as green while every shipped binary still carried it. Only the release-time binary scan could catch that, which means it was caught at the last possible moment or not at all. Resolution now goes through one composite action (`.github/actions/go-toolchain`) that reads the version from `dev/tools/verify-go-toolchain --print`, exports `GOTOOLCHAIN` job-wide — an exact pin, where go.mod's directive is only a *minimum* and leaves a runner one patch ahead running ahead — and then asserts `go env GOVERSION` matches before the job proceeds, so a wrong toolchain is a failed step rather than a green scan of the wrong thing. Sourcing `dev/tools/common.sh` applies the same pin locally, so a contributor's `verify-vuln` and CI's now describe the same stdlib. Two more instances of the original misread went with it: `release.yml` — the workflow that publishes the **binaries** — was still doing `awk '/^go /' go.mod`, reading the compatibility floor exactly as `release-images.yml` had, and `release-images.yml` set up a hardcoded `'1.26'` for its artifact-inspection step. The `go toolchain` check grew the guardrails to match: no workflow may call `actions/setup-go` directly or define its own `GO_VERSION`, and the composite action must still run both `--print` and `--running`. It stays pure grep with no Go setup of its own, so it remains cheap enough to run unconditionally on every PR.
- ci: **published images now build on the toolchain we pin, and the artifact is checked before it ships.** `release-images.yml` derived its `golang:<version>-alpine` builder from go.mod's `go` directive — the language compatibility *floor* (`1.26.4`) — rather than `toolchain` (`1.26.6`), so the bump immediately above this entry turned `govulncheck` green on the source tree while every image published from that same commit was still compiled against the stdlib it was bumped to escape. Ten advisories were reachable in the shipped binary at the moment CI reported zero: GO-2026-4970, -5026, -5856, -5942, -5972, -6088, -6089, -6090, -6091, -6218. Nothing in the pipeline could have noticed — source-mode `govulncheck` scans the tree with whatever toolchain `setup-go` resolves on the runner, never the one inside the build container, and the official `golang` images set `GOTOOLCHAIN=local`, so go.mod's `toolchain` line can't rescue a build that starts from the wrong base image either. The version now resolves through a single shared reader (`dev/tools/verify-go-toolchain --print`, `toolchain` preferred, `go` as fallback for a tidy that drops a redundant line), and two gates keep the drift from re-opening: a new `go toolchain` check requires both Dockerfiles' `ARG GO_VERSION` defaults (stale at `1.26.3`, which is what a bare local `docker build` was silently using) and the release workflow itself to agree with go.mod, and the release job now builds `linux/amd64`, **exports the image filesystem, and verifies the binary that will actually be published** — its recorded build-info Go version must match go.mod, and its stdlib must carry no advisories — *before* the multi-arch push, so a mismatch fails the release rather than shipping it. The artifact scan is deliberately scoped to stdlib: binary-mode `govulncheck` has no call-graph reachability, so it over-reports third-party modules (15 x/crypto + x/net findings today that source mode correctly rules unreachable) while judging the stdlib exactly, since that version comes from the binary's own build info. The pre-merge check runs in its own workflow with no `paths` filter, because ci.yml's `paths-ignore` skips `Dockerfile` and `release*.yml` — precisely the files it polices — and an allowlist filter would leave a required check permanently "skipped" on unrelated PRs. Fixes [#736](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/736).
- ci: bump the `toolchain` directive to **go1.26.6**, which clears the seven standard-library advisories `govulncheck` was failing main on (GO-2026-6218 `net/url`, -6091 `html/template`, -6090 `crypto/tls`, -6089 and -5026 `net/http`, -6088 `encoding/xml`, -5972 `encoding/asn1` — every one "fixed in go1.26.6"). The workflow asks `setup-go` for `1.26` with `check-latest`, but the runner image's tool cache still resolves that to 1.26.5, so the pin has to live in `go.mod`, where `GOTOOLCHAIN=auto` makes it authoritative for CI and for every contributor's local presubmit run alike. No source change; the language version (`go 1.26.4`) is untouched, so the supported-compiler floor is unchanged.
- ci: `dev/tools/lint-go` gains `funlen` + `gocognit` as a **ratchet** — the lint set had no complexity or length linter at all, so nothing structurally prevented the next god-method from accreting. Thresholds are pinned to the measured in-tree maximum on the day they were set (254 lines / 145 statements / 110 cognitive complexity), which is the point: the whole existing tree passes with zero code changes and the only thing that can fail is a function worse than anything we already have. A conventional threshold would have manufactured a refactor backlog nobody asked for; the goal here is purely preventing regression, and the numbers are meant to be lowered as hotspots land ([#659](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/659)'s acceptance criteria include dropping them a notch) and never raised. Measuring turned up one thing the assessment missed: `cmd/core-agent`'s `run` is a **1228-line / 642-statement / 374-complexity** outlier — 5× worse than the next function in the tree on every metric, and roughly 8× the `Agent.Run()` the assessment named as the worst offender. Pinning above it would have left a fence at 1229 lines, which forbids nothing anyone would write by accident, so that one function is excluded by name in the config next to the numbers it distorts, rather than as a scattered `//nolint` that goes stale invisibly. The exclusion is function-scoped, not file-scoped, so a *new* god-method elsewhere in `main.go` still fails; decomposing `run` and dropping the exemption is [#685](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/685). Closes [#662](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/662).
- compose/session: `pkg/compose` — the substrate every embedder builds on — goes from **62.0% to 83.7%** statement coverage with no function left at 0%, and the deprecated `pkg/session` shim from 0% to 100%. Writing the tests found three live defects, each gated by a test that fails on pre-fix code. (1) `CfgToCatalogOverride` dropped `CachedInputPerMTok` while `pkg/usage`'s `cfgToOverride` — the same translation on the path taken when no catalog is installed — carries it, so an operator's configured cache-read rate was honored under test and silently discarded in the daemon (`cmd/core-agent/main.go` builds the catalog through the compose helper), billing every cached input token at the full input rate; the new `TestCfgOverride_MatchesTheNoCatalogPath` prices the same config through both paths and requires them to agree. (2) `formatMCPLine`'s nil guard sat *inside* the render loop, below a `sort.Slice` comparator that dereferences `.Name` — so the guard was unreachable and a nil server entry panicked the startup summary instead of being skipped; nils are now dropped before the sort, and an all-nil slice reads as `0 servers loaded` rather than a dangling `— `. (3) `summarizeRefreshOutcome` had no `Skipped` arm despite a doc comment claiming it renders "the same four-shape outcome as `DescribeRefresh`", so a skipped refresh fell through to the default arm and reported "updated N models from upstream" for a call that fetched nothing. Also raises the CI coverage floor 62.0% → 67.0% (measured total 68.1%) to keep the gain. Closes [#663](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/663).
- examples: a new `examples/internal/recipecheck` gate fails the build when a recipe's **content names something its own config cannot produce**. Recipe tests until now validated structure — the config parses, the skills load, the MCP block is well-formed — which is exactly why [#644](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/644) shipped: a `k8s-triage` skill full of `kubectl` runbooks in a recipe whose `tools.disable` removes `bash`, structurally valid and operationally impossible. The checker reads reachability off the real boot sequence (`tools.Default()` → `Disable` loop → `tools.Build`) rather than a reimplementation, so conditional registration — `fetch_url` needs a URL allowlist, `alert` needs a target, `record_plan` needs `require_plan_artifact` — is answered by the same code the daemon runs and cannot drift from it. Six rules, all offline and credential-free: a built-in named in skill prose that the config doesn't register (with the *reason* it isn't: disable list, missing precondition, …); a shell command (`kubectl`, `gcloud`, `helm`, `docker`, `terraform`, `curl`, `istioctl`) named while `bash` is unregistered; an MCP name written with a double underscore (`gke__get_pod` — `pkg/mcp/namespace.go` joins with one); a name in an unambiguous tool position (`tool: "X"`) that resolves to nothing; a `wait_and_verify.poll_allow` entry matching no tool, which is silently refused at call time rather than rejected at load; and a `wait_and_verify` target absent from `poll_allow`. It deliberately does *not* try to prove every snake_case token is a tool — config keys are shaped identically — so it checks only what is decidable from the config alone. Recipes are **discovered** by walking `examples/` for config roots, so a new one is covered the day it lands and nothing has to be registered. Waivers exist for vendored trees the repo does not edit, require a written reason, and log their count on every run; `kube-platform-agent`'s two kube-agents snapshots are waived under [#674](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/674). Closes [#645](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/645).

## [2.8.0] — 2026-08-07

v2.8.0 makes core-agent restart-resilient, fully observable, and embeddable. The marquee change is unattended durability: a turn interrupted by a pod roll or crash is now finished automatically on the next boot — **on by default** for durable multi-session and headless `--no-repl` daemons (`--session-db`) — and stranded continuations self-heal in-lifetime via a bounded retry loop, backed by MAX_TOKENS-resume and SQLite `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` write-path fixes. Observability reaches full coverage (#325 / #338): OTel metrics — `core_agent.*` subsystem meters, ADK-schema GenAI histograms, and Go runtime instrumentation — export over OTLP push and Prometheus pull, and outbound Gemini LLM calls emit traced HTTP-client spans. The bundled daemon's substrate becomes reusable library surface via the `pkg/compose` extraction (#386) and the promoted, stable `pkg/pricing` (#489), so downstreams assemble the same stack the binary does. Attach adds default-on per-caller cost rate limiting, an SSE v1.4.0 capability frame, REST response-shape conformance pins, and fixes for three session-lifecycle edge cases (#565 / #566 / #230); model defaults advance to `gemini-3.6-flash` (zero-config), `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` (cheap tier), and first-class Claude 5 (`opus-5` / `fable-5`) support. There are no breaking changes; the one migration note is that `agent.auto_continue.enabled` is now a `*bool` tristate — omit it for the new precondition-gated default, or set `false` to opt out.

### Changes by Kind

#### Feature
- config: the zero-config default model is now `gemini-3.6-flash` (was `gemini-3.1-pro-preview-customtools`). It is a current-generation, generally-available flash model — the taskclass `frontier` tier — that combines server-side search built-ins with function tools out of the box, a better first impression than the prior 3.1-pro *preview* / custom-tools build, while satisfying Gemini's "3.0-or-later required when combining built-ins with function tools" constraint so zero-config users need not think about it. Override `model.name` for a pro-class model, or the `gemini-3.1-pro-preview-customtools` variant (prefers registered tools over raw bash) when you want that behavior; the default will revisit `gemini-3.6-pro` when it ships. Closes [#571](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/571).
- auto-continue: interrupted turns are now finished automatically **by default** on daemons that can use the feature — a multi-session daemon or a headless `--no-repl` single-user daemon, both with a durable eventlog (`--session-db`). `agent.auto_continue.enabled` became a `*bool` tristate: omit it for the precondition-gated default (on for those daemons, off — silently — for interactive REPL/TUI runs and in-process library callers, which the precondition excludes so they never auto-continue by surprise), set an explicit `false` to opt out, or `true` to force it on (warned-and-ignored where it cannot apply). The daemon logs a one-line notice when it turns on by default. This flips the v2.8 opt-in / off-by-default posture now that the guard stack has had production soak. Closes [#559](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/559). (#583)
- auto-continue: a stranded continuation now self-heals in-lifetime. A background retry driver (`AutoContinueRetryLoop`, default-on wherever auto-continue is enabled; interval `agent.auto_continue.retry_interval`, default `5m`, opt out with `retry: false`) re-runs the guarded pass on a timer instead of waiting for a reboot or the next human message, so a transiently-failed continuation (momentary run-lock contention, a provider blip, a residual DB hiccup) recovers on a long-lived daemon. It stays bounded by the same crash-loop breaker and per-session cumulative cap, and — running off the daemon's own context — a continuation that kills the daemon kills the driver too, so only survivable failures ever re-fire. Outcome-aware boot-log accounting also refunds fleet lock-losers, so the N−1 daemons that lose the run lock while sharing one eventlog no longer each burn the shared per-session attempt cap. Fixes [#575](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/575) (defect B). (#577)
- models/gemini/taskclass: the gemini cheap-tier default moves from `gemini-2.5-flash` to `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` (released 2026-07-21) — same price point ($0.30/$2.50 per MTok), current generation, far stronger agentic scores, March 2026 cutoff.
